\dim c^{\prime}=i\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{c^{\prime}}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c_{2})}

is a direct sum of morphisms

R_{c_{1},c_{2}}^{c^{\prime}}:\Lambda_{c^{\prime}}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c_{1})}\longrightarrow\Lambda_{c^{\prime}}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c_{2})}

which are simply restriction of functions in the second coordinate. We obtain the following result by applying the residue resolution (Proposition
8.5
) after taking the tensor product with
\mathcal{O}_{T}
.

Lemma 9.18
.

There is a triple complex of sheaves which is
F_{\mathrm{irr}}
-filtered quasi-isomorphic to
\Lambda^{\bullet}
which is depicted in Figure
4
. The horizontal morphisms are direct sums of morphisms described in (
35
) and the vertical morphisms are induced by a direct sum of the residue morphisms (
36
) taken with respect to a signed incidence relation.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{0_{N}}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\dim c^{\prime}=1\end{subarray}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{\dim c^{\prime}=1}\Lambda_{0_{N}}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\dim c^{\prime}=2\end{subarray}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\dim c^{\prime}=2\\
\dim c=1\\
c\subseteq c^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{c}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{\dim c^{\prime}=2}\Lambda_{0_{N}}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\dim c^{\prime}=3\\
\dim c=2\\
c\subseteq c^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{c}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{{\begin{subarray}{c}\dim c^{\prime}=3\\
\dim c=1\\
c\subseteq c^{\prime}\end{subarray}}}\Lambda_{c}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{\dim c^{\prime}=3}\Lambda_{0_{N}}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}

Figure 4.

In particular, after a shift, the resolution described in Lemma
9.18
is the total complex of a double complex of the form

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\dim c^{\prime}=\dim c\\
c\subseteq c^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{c}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c^{\prime})}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\dim c^{\prime}=\dim c+1\\
c\subseteq c^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{c}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c^{\prime})}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\dim c^{\prime}=\dim c+2\\
c\subseteq c^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{c}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c^{\prime})}\longrightarrow\dots

Finally, each filtered complex object appearing in Figure
4
may be replaced by the filtered complex
{\bf s\Lambda}^{\bullet}_{c}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T(c^{\prime})}
. This then becomes a complex whose
p
-th term is of the form

\bigoplus_{a+b=p}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\dim c^{\prime}=\dim c+a\\
\dim{\sigma}-\dim c^{\prime}=b\\
\mathrm{rec}(\sigma)=c^{\prime}\\
c\subseteq c^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{(c,\sigma)}

The sum is taken over all possible
c,c^{\prime}
and
\sigma
satisfying the given numerical conditions and so that
\mathrm{rec}(\sigma)\in\Sigma_{D,0}
. The expression above simplifies to

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c,\sigma)\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
\dim\sigma-\dim c=p\end{subarray}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{(c,\sigma)}

Taking care to keep track of the morphisms and signs appearing in this total complex, this proves the following result.

Proposition 9.19
.

There is a
F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{\bullet}
-filtered resolution of
\Lambda^{\bullet}
given by the total complex of the following double complex;

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c,\sigma)\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
\dim\sigma-\dim c=0\end{subarray}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{(c,\sigma)}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c,\sigma)\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
\dim\sigma-\dim c=1\end{subarray}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{(c,\sigma)}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c,\sigma)\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
\dim\sigma-\dim c=2\end{subarray}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{(c,\sigma)}\longrightarrow\dots

There is a choice of signed incidence relation on
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D},0}
so that the
i
th differential of this complex is the matrix whose entries are

[(c,\sigma):(c^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})]\cdot\alpha((c,\sigma),(c^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})).

for all pairs
(c,\sigma)
so that
\dim\sigma-\dim c=i
and
\dim\sigma^{\prime}-\dim c^{\prime}=i+1
.

Remark 9.20
.

All of the resolutions in this section can be viewed as termwise resolutions of the sheaves
\Lambda^{p}
or even as resolutions of
\Omega^{p}_{T/\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\log D\cup T_{0})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T_{0}}
. These resolutions commute with
d+dw_{\sigma}
and with
F^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{irr}}
to produce resolutions of filtered complexes.

Finally, we prove the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 9.21
.

Suppose
(T(\Sigma),D,w_{\varphi},\pi)
is a quasi-stable degeneration of toric Landau–Ginzburg models as constructed in Proposition
9.4
, and that the monomial support of
w_{\sigma}
is the vertex set of a simplex for all
\sigma
. Then:

(1)

Property
8.16
holds for
(T(\Sigma),D,w_{\varphi},\pi)
.

(2)

There is a filtered isomorphism

(\mathbb{H}^{n}(T,\Lambda^{\bullet}),F^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{irr}})\cong\bigoplus_{p+q=n}(H^{p}(\mathsf{T}_{\Sigma}(\Sigma_{0,D},w_{\varphi})_{0},\tilde{\bf J}^{q}),\mathsf{F}^{\bullet}).

Proof.

According to Proposition
9.19
,
\mathbb{H}^{n}(T,F^{\lambda}_{\mathrm{irr}}\Lambda^{\bullet})
is the hypercohomology of

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c,\sigma)\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
\dim\sigma-\dim c=0\end{subarray}}F^{\lambda}_{\mathrm{irr}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{(c,\sigma)}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c,\sigma)\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
\dim\sigma-\dim c=1\end{subarray}}F^{\lambda}_{\mathrm{irr}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{(c,\sigma)}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c,\sigma)\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
\dim\sigma-\dim c=2\end{subarray}}F^{\lambda}_{\mathrm{irr}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{(c,\sigma)}\longrightarrow\dots.

By Theorem
3.11
and (
30
), there is a functorial quasi-isomorphism
\mathbb{R}\Gamma(F^{\lambda}_{\mathrm{irr}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{(c,\sigma)})\cong\Gamma(F^{\lambda}_{\mathrm{irr}}\Lambda_{(c,\sigma)}^{\bullet})
and by (
32
) and Proposition
9.13
there is a functorial quasi-isomorphism between
\Gamma(F^{\lambda}_{\mathrm{irr}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{(c,\sigma)})
and the trivial complex with trivial differential whose
p
-th component is
\mathsf{F}^{\lambda-p}{\bf B}_{\tau_{\sigma}}\otimes\wedge^{p-\dim\tau_{\sigma}}c^{\perp}\otimes\mathrm{Vol}(L(\tau_{\sigma}))=\mathsf{F}^{\lambda}\tilde{\bf J}^{p}{(c,\sigma)}
. The rows of this double complex are of the form:

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{cell}}^{p}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{F}^{\lambda}\tilde{\bf J}^{\bullet}):=

(37)

\displaystyle\left[\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c,\sigma)\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
\dim\sigma-\dim c=0\end{subarray}}\mathsf{F}^{\lambda}\tilde{\bf J}^{p}{(c,\sigma)}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c,\sigma)\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
\dim\sigma-\dim c=1\end{subarray}}\mathsf{F}^{\lambda}\tilde{\bf J}^{p}{(c,\sigma)}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c,\sigma)\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
\dim\sigma-\dim c=2\end{subarray}}\mathsf{F}^{\lambda}\tilde{\bf J}^{p}{(c,\sigma)}\longrightarrow\dots\right]

where morphisms are matrices of maps whose entries are identified with the morphisms induced by
[(c,\sigma):(c^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})]\alpha((c,\sigma),(c^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}))
. By the discussion in Section
9.4
, specifically (
33
), these morphisms are simply
\tilde{\bf J}^{p}((c,\sigma),(c^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}))
when restricted to
\mathrm{im}(\xi_{(c,\sigma)})
(see (
32
) for the notation). Consequently, they are graded morphisms and in particular,
\mathsf{F}^{\lambda}
-strict. Therefore, the inclusion
\mathsf{F}^{\lambda}\tilde{\bf J}^{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\tilde{\bf J}^{\bullet}
induces an inclusion in cohomology. By the argument above, this inclusion is identified with the morphism
\mathbb{H}^{n}(T,F^{\lambda}_{\mathrm{irr}}\Lambda^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{n}(T,\Lambda^{\bullet})
. This proves the first statement.

To prove the second statement, we identify (
37
) with
C_{\mathrm{cell}}^{\bullet}(\mathsf{T}(\Sigma_{0,D},w_{\varphi})_{0},\tilde{\bf J}^{p})
. Therefore,
\mathbb{H}^{n}(T,\Lambda^{\bullet})
is isomorphic to the cohomology of

\bigoplus_{p}C^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{cell}}(\mathsf{T}(\Sigma_{0,D},w_{\varphi})_{0},\tilde{\bf J}^{p})[p]

which implies that
\mathbb{H}^{n}(T,\Lambda^{\bullet})\cong\oplus_{p+q=n}H^{q}(\mathsf{T}(\Sigma_{0,D},w_{\varphi})_{0},\tilde{\bf J}^{p})
. The second statement then follows by application of Proposition
9.15
.
∎

Finally, we arrive at the main result of this section by combining Theorem
9.21
and Proposition
9.15
with Proposition
8.16
.

Corollary 9.22
.

Suppose
(T(\Sigma),D,w_{\varphi},\pi)
is a simplicial quasi-stable degeneration of toric Landau–Ginzburg models and choose some
t
so that
1\gg|t|>0
. Then

\dim{\mathrm{gr}}_{F_{\mathrm{irr}}}^{\lambda}H^{n}(T(\Sigma)_{t}\setminus D_{t},w_{\varphi,t})=\sum_{p+q=n}{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathsf{F}}^{\lambda}H^{q}(\mathsf{T}(\Sigma_{0,D},w_{\varphi})_{0},{\bf J}^{p}).

Remark 9.23

(Orbifold cohomology)
.

It is straightforward to generalize Theorem
9.21
and Corollary
9.22
to the orbifold cohomology. For a quasistable degeneration of Landau–Ginzburg models
(X,D,w,\pi)
, the relevant twisted sectors are parametrized by a relative inertia stack
\mathfrak{I}_{\pi,X}
that is a substack of
\mathfrak{I}_{X}
defined by the union of components over which the restriction of
\pi
is flat. Geometrically, each component in
\mathfrak{I}_{\pi,X}
corresponds to a quasi-stable degeneration of the corresponding twisted sector, hence we apply the same argument above to compute the irregular Hodge numbers of the nearby fibre in each twisted sector.

9.6.
Application to Clarke dual pairs

The very last observation that we need to make is that the constructions in this section may be applied in the case of Clarke dual pairs of Landau–Ginzburg models. Recall that we are given a pair
({\bf\Sigma},\check{\bf\Sigma})
satisfying Definition
5.2
. We let
A={\bf\check{\Sigma}}[1]\cup\{0\}
and note that
\Delta_{\bf\check{\Sigma}}=\mathrm{Conv}(A)
. Since
{\bf\check{\Sigma}}
is quasiprojective and convex we are given a star triangulation based at
0_{N}
by the function
\check{\varphi}
(Proposition
5.12
). The condition that
\langle n,m\rangle\geq 0
for
n\in\mathrm{Supp}(\check{\Sigma})
and
m\in\mathrm{Supp}(\Sigma)
implies that the simplicial fan
{\Sigma}
is contained in
\mathrm{nc}(0_{N})
. We would like
{\Sigma}
to play the role of
\Sigma_{0,D}
in this situation, which requires that we extend
{\Sigma}
to a subfan of a refinement of
\mathrm{nf}(\Delta_{\bf\check{\Sigma}})
.

Proposition 9.24
.

Suppose
({\bf\Sigma},\check{\bf\Sigma})
form a Clarke dual pair of fans. Then there is a simplicial fan
\Sigma_{0}
so that

(1)

\Sigma_{0}
is a refinement of
\mathrm{nf}(\Delta_{\check{\bf\Sigma}})
,

(2)

\Sigma
is a subfan of
\Sigma_{0}
,

(3)

T(\Sigma_{0})\setminus T(\Sigma)
is the support of a divisor.

Proof.

This construction is perhaps easiest to explain geometrically. Since
\Sigma
is quasiprojective, the toric variety
T(\Sigma)
may be compactified to a toric subvariety
\overline{T}
of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
for some integer
n
, and whose image is the complement of a collection of divisors. By blowing up repeatedly in singular strata, we may assume that
\overline{T}
has at worst orbifold singularities. This blow-up avoids
T({\Sigma})\subseteq\overline{T}
because
T(\Sigma)
itself has at worst orbifold singularities. The zero-locus of
w(\check{\bf\Sigma})
, denoted
Z
, is not necessarily a nondegenerate hypersurface in
\overline{T}
. However, for each open toric stratum
B
of
\overline{T}
, either
Z\cap B
is smooth or
B\subseteq Z
because coefficients for
w(\check{\bf\Sigma})
are chosen generically. After iterated blow-up at those strata
B\subseteq Z
, we may assume
Z\cap B
is smooth for all
B
. Let
{T}_{0}
be such a variety. Since
\check{\Sigma}\subseteq\mathrm{nc}(0_{M})
, the intersection of
Z
and all open toric strata in
T({\Sigma})
is smooth, so this iterated blow up again does not affect
T({\Sigma})\subseteq\overline{T}
. Let
{\Sigma}_{0}
denote the fan attached to
{T}_{0}
. The fact that
Z\cap B
is smooth for all open toric strata is equivalent to item (1). Item (2) holds because
T(\Sigma)
is an open subvariety of
T_{0}
.

By construction,
T\setminus T(\Sigma)
is a divisor. The blow up process does not change this. Therefore,
T^{\prime}\setminus T(\Sigma)
is also a collection of divisors. Therefore, item (3) holds.
∎
